For Germany, the financial wealth of households aged 55 and older can only be estimated approximately on the basis

of household surveys. According to the latest Panel on Household Finances (PHF) survey, published by the Bundesbank⁶,
mean gross wealth reached around EUR114,400 for households whose reference person was aged 55–64, EUR98,200
for those aged 65–74 and EUR107,900 for those aged 75 and older. The comparatively lower figure for the 65–74 age
group may partly reflect the weaker labor-market experience of cohorts born between 1950 and 1958, who were
exposed to high unemployment and subdued real wage growth during important parts of their working lives. Combining

these wealth estimates with the number of households in each age group reported in the Mikrozensus⁷ , we estimate
that the cohorts aged 55 and older hold around 60% of the private households’ gross financial assets, which would
correspond to around EUR 6.1trn in 2025 (see Figure 19).
Including real estate, average household net wealth in Germany stood at EUR324,000 in 2023, slightly above the
euro-area average of EUR312,000⁸. Wealth peaked among households whose reference person was aged 55–64, at

EUR440,100 on average. It remained high among those aged 65–74, at EUR411,400, before declining to EUR343,700 for
households aged 75 and older. By contrast, households headed by someone aged 16–34 held average net wealth of just
EUR106,900.
These differences largely reflect the typical lifecycle of wealth accumulation. Households build up assets during their
working lives and, on average, begin to draw them down in retirement. The same pattern can be observed across most

EU countries, although the pace of decumulation in old age differs markedly depending on the generosity and financing
structure of public pension systems (Figure 20).

These figures should nevertheless be interpreted with caution. Household wealth surveys tend to understate financial
assets, while the very wealthiest households are often underrepresented. In Germany, for example, the PHF survey
puts average gross financial assets at EUR87,400 per household, compared with EUR117,890 based on the aggregate
household balance sheet. Survey-based estimates of future inheritances should therefore be regarded as indicative

rather than precise.
Moreover, the wealth that younger generations may inherit over the coming decades represents an upper bound
rather than a guaranteed transfer. Wealth is distributed very unevenly: in Germany, the richest 10% own 53.7% of total
net wealth⁹. DIW estimates suggest that around EUR400bn is inherited or gifted each year, with roughly half of these
transfers accruing to the wealthiest 10% of beneficiaries10. At the same time, rising health and long-term care costs,

together with less generous pension systems, are likely to absorb a growing share of household wealth before it can be
transferred. The coming inheritance wave will therefore be substantial, but highly uneven, and could reinforce existing
wealth inequalities within younger generations.
The same applies in the US. Consider a single retiree who wants to withdraw an average of EUR2,000 per month to
cover living, health and care costs. Assuming average inflation of 3.0%, an annual return of 5.0% and a retirement period

of 17.4 years – the average remaining life expectancy of a 70-year-old woman according to the latest UN population
statistics – the required savings at age 70 would amount to around EUR368,700. This is equivalent to roughly 46% of
the calculated average net wealth of EUR802,800 for the 70+ age group. A substantial share of accumulated wealth
therefore needs to remain available to finance retirement itself rather than being passed on.
Combined with the highly unequal distribution of US household wealth, this helps explain why estimates of the “Great

Wealth Transfer” vary so widely, from around USD36trn to more than USD100trn over the coming decades.11 Headline
asset stocks therefore provide only a rough indication of the wealth that will ultimately be transferred to younger
generations.
Nevertheless, the broader conclusion remains: the intergenerational debate should consider both sides of the ledger.
Younger generations will face rising public-sector burdens as societies age, but they will also receive one of the largest

private wealth transfers in history. Neither offsets the other. But discussing future pension, health and long-term
care liabilities without considering the parallel transfer of private assets gives an incomplete picture of the economic
relationship between generations.

Households deleverage – governments do not
The global household-debt ratio fell to 60.9% of GDP
in 2025, almost 10pps below its 2005 level and nearly
13pps below its 2009 peak (Figure 21). This does not
mean that households stopped borrowing: liabilities
continued to increase, but more slowly than nominal
economic output. Nevertheless, the contrast with the
public sector is striking. While government debt has
continued to rise, households have spent much of the

past decade strengthening their balance sheets.
But deleveraging was far from universal. Since 2005, the
debt ratio has fallen by 20.4pps in North America and
by 6.3pps in Western Europe, while Japan’s ratio has
remained broadly stable. Australia and New Zealand
stand apart from this trend: their combined ratio rose by
12.2pps to 113.8%, although it remains below its 2020
peak. The decline in the global ratio therefore conceals
a significant shift in the geography of household debt,

from the traditional advanced economies towards
emerging markets, albeit with important exceptions.
China recorded by far the largest increase. Its
household-debt ratio rose by 42.1pps to 59.4% of GDP
between 2005 and 2025, reflecting the extraordinary
credit and property boom of the intervening years.
However, the ratio has barely changed since 2020,
underlining how abruptly that boom has ended. China’s
household-debt ratio is now only around 10pps below

that of the US, but the two countries have followed
opposite paths: American households have deleveraged
substantially, while China has moved from rapid debt
accumulation to balance-sheet caution.
Elsewhere in the emerging world, the increase was less
dramatic. Since 2005, the household-debt ratio has
risen by 10.7pps in Latin America to 27.8% and by 6.1pps
in Eastern Europe to 21.8%. Both remain moderate by
international standards. Asia excluding Japan and China

also appears relatively stable, with its aggregate ratio
rising by only 2.2pps to 55.1%. But this regional average
is deceptive. It partly reflects the growing weight
of poorer, less-indebted economies and substantial
deleveraging in Singapore, where the ratio fell by
26.4pps to 51.6%.
At the country level, the fault lines cut across the
advanced and emerging-market divide. Switzerland and
Australia recorded debt ratios of 125.1% and 119.4% of
GDP , respectively, while Canada stood at 103.3% and

the Netherlands at 97.2%. Several Asian economies
also carry substantial household-debt burdens: Taiwan
and South Korea recorded ratios of around 92%, while
Thailand and Malaysia stood at approximately 87%
and 85%, respectively. These levels are comparable
with those recorded in several Western economies
before the global financial crisis. In 2006, household
debt stood at 98.4% of GDP in the US, 84.2% in Spain
and 95.0% in Ireland. High leverage does not in itself

signal an imminent crisis, but it makes households and
consumption more vulnerable to income, interest-rate
and housing-market shocks. The decisive dividing line is
therefore not between advanced and emerging markets,
but between housing and financial systems that rely
heavily on household leverage and those that do not.

Strong asset growth and subdued borrowing pushed

global net financial assets (financial assets minus
liabilities) up by +10.1% in 2025, broadly matching
the previous year’s +10.2%. They reached a record
EUR211.5trn, almost twice their 2015 level and more
than tripling their value in 2005.
China recorded the strongest increase in net financial
wealth at +15.3%, reflecting the combination of strong
asset growth and the near-standstill in household debt.
Latin America followed at +11.3%, while Eastern Europe

and Asia excluding China and Japan both recorded
growth of +11.0%. North America and Australia and
New Zealand also achieved double-digit increases
of +10.4% each. Japan recorded a strong +7.4%, while
Western Europe again brought up the rear at +5.2%.
Growth exceeded its 20-year average in almost every
region (Figure 22). Latin America was the only exception,
although its +11.3% increase was broadly in line with its
long-term rate.
The figures reveal a less visible source of wealth

creation. Weak credit growth may constrain housing
activity and household investment, but it also
strengthens private balance sheets when financial
assets continue to expand. In 2025, this deleveraging
dividend lifted global net financial wealth faster than
gross financial wealth. It also reinforced North America’s
dominant position: the region held 51.3% of global net
financial assets, compared with 48.1% of gross financial
assets. Low leverage relative to its enormous asset base

gives North America an additional wealth advantage.
Adjusted for population growth and inflation, China
remains the undisputed long-term leader (Figure 23).
Real net financial assets per capita have increased
more than sevenfold over the past two decades. No
other region comes close. Even the other emerging-
market regions recorded little more than a doubling.
Among advanced markets, Western Europe continued
to lag behind Japan, highlighting how weak asset

growth, rather than debt accumulation, has constrained
European wealth creation.
Comparing gross and net financial assets adds another
dimension. In China, net financial assets grew around
0.7pps more slowly per year than gross financial
assets over the past two decades, reflecting the rapid
accumulation of household debt. A smaller gap is
also visible in Eastern Europe. However, this is not a
universal emerging-market pattern: in Latin America
and the rest of Asia, gross and net wealth developed at

broadly similar rates. In the advanced economies, the
relationship is reversed. As household debt generally
grew more slowly than assets, net financial wealth
outpaced gross wealth. The difference was particularly
pronounced in North America, where net financial assets
grew around 0.7pps faster per year.

Examining the 25 richest countries by net financial assets
per capita at the end of 2025 reveals how significantly
debt can alter the ranking (Figure 24). Most notably,
the US ranks ahead of Switzerland to claim first place.
Although Switzerland remains ahead in gross financial
assets, its household liabilities of around EUR130,080
per capita are more than twice the US figure of
EUR52,730.
Taiwan and Sweden exchange fifth and sixth places.

Australia falls from eighth place in the gross ranking to
eleventh in the net ranking, while Ireland slips from 14th
to 15th, the UK from 15th to 18th and Norway from 12th
to 22nd. By contrast, Germany rises from 13th to 12th,
Italy from 16th to 13th, Japan from 18th to 14th and
Spain from 22nd to 21st. The Netherlands remains ninth
in both rankings. The comparison makes clear that gross
wealth tells only half the story: what ultimately matters
is how much remains after debt.

After a temporary acceleration to +4.7% in 2024,
growth in global real estate assets12 slowed again to
+3.5% in 2025. Apart from 2023, growth was weaker
only in the aftermath of the global financial crisis in
2012. Persistently high construction costs and elevated
mortgage rates continued to weigh on affordability,
transactions and new development. Overall, the value of
real estate assets in the countries covered by the report

reached EUR155.0trn (Figure 25)
Regional developments varied widely (Figure 26).
Eastern Europe recorded the strongest increase at
+10.6%, followed by Australia and New Zealand at
+8.5%. Both comfortably exceeded their long-term
growth rates despite the pressure from higher financing
costs. Japan also stood out: real-estate assets increased
by +5.0%, compared with a long-term average of
just +0.7%. Together with the recent acceleration in
household borrowing, this provides further evidence that

Japan may finally be leaving its long deflationary period
behind.
Western Europe recorded growth of +4.1%, slightly
above its long-term average of +3.5%. North America
meanwhile was the clear exception: growth slowed
sharply from +4.9% in 2024 to just +1.8% in 2025,
less than half its long-term rate of +3.9%. The global
slowdown was therefore driven primarily by the largest
housing market.
Real estate:
A hesitant recovery

and Latin America are therefore not represented. For Western Europe, data for Greece, Portugal, Malta and Ireland are missing. The aggregate for

34
As with financial assets, a clearer picture emerges
when real-estate wealth is measured per capita and
adjusted for inflation (Figure 27). The long shadow of
Japan’s property crash remains visible: over the past two
decades, real-estate wealth per capita was essentially
flat in real terms, despite the recent recovery.
Real gains were positive in Australia and New Zealand,
North America and Western Europe, but generally
modest. Compared with financial assets, the gap was

particularly pronounced in North America, where real
financial assets per capita grew around 2pps faster
per year, and in Japan, where the gap was around
1.3pps. In Australia and New Zealand and Western
Europe, by contrast, the difference was only around
0.2pps annually. Property may provide stability, housing
services and diversification, but capital appreciation
alone has generally been a weaker engine of wealth
creation than participation in financial markets.

The country ranking of real estate assets nevertheless
shows how strongly housing can reshape the overall
picture. Switzerland had the highest real-estate assets
per capita at EUR350,450, followed by Australia
at EUR279,310 with the US taking the 7th spot at
EUR174,030. But what does the ranking look like when
net financial assets and real estate assets are presented
together? Figure 28 provides the answer. Switzerland
overtakes the US and claims first place, with combined

wealth of around EUR626,430 per capita. Australia
meanwhile is the biggest beneficiary, climbing seven
places from tenth in the adjusted net-financial-assets
ranking to third once property is included. Sweden
and Japan each fall six places, while Italy drops four.
Germany moves up one position to tenth. These shifts
expose two very different models of household wealth:
some countries build wealth predominantly through
financial markets, while others hold a much larger share

in bricks and mortar.

International: Catch-up loses momentum
For many years, global wealth development was
characterized by convergence, as emerging markets
gradually closed the gap with richer economies. That
process has not ended, but it has slowed dramatically.
The ratio of average net financial assets per capita in
advanced economies to those in emerging markets
fell from 62 in 2005 to 21 in 2015. This means that, on
average, the net financial assets of richer countries were

“only” 21 times higher than those of poorer countries in
2015. Over the following decade, however, it declined by
less than four points to 17 in 2025 (Figure 29).
Convergence reversed from 2017. After narrowing
rapidly for more than a decade, the wealth gap widened
again between 2017 and 2021. Convergence resumed
in 2022, but at a much slower pace: the ratio has fallen
from 18.5 to 17.2 in the past three years. The global
wealth gap is still closing, but has slowed to a crawl.

Declaring the end of globalization would be premature,
but its character has clearly changed. Geopolitical
tensions, weaker cross-border investment and the
reorganization of supply chains are eroding some of
the advantages that emerging markets derived from
ever-deeper global integration. At the same time, the
green transition and AI could create new opportunities
by increasing demand for critical resources, energy
infrastructure, data services and digitally delivered

work. Whether emerging economies can seize these
opportunities will depend on their access to technology,
capital and skills. For now, these transformations are
unfolding in a more fragmented world, creating a less
favorable environment for poorer countries to catch up.
Against this backdrop, the global wealth middle class
comprised around 866mn people in 2025.13 Although
its size changed little compared with the previous
year, its long-term expansion remains impressive:

membership has increased by 81% since 2005. More
importantly, its center of gravity has shifted. Emerging
markets accounted for 61.9% of its members in 2025,
up from 43.6% two decades earlier, with China alone
representing slightly more than half of the emerging-
market middle class.
Distribution:
New wealth, old divides

The transformation is even more striking among the
wealthy. The global high-wealth class grew by 37%

to around 584mn people between 2005 and 2025.
Emerging markets accounted for 32.1% of this group,
compared with less than 2% in 2005. This expansion
came entirely from emerging markets, while the number
of high-wealth individuals in advanced economies
declined slightly. China played the dominant role,
accounting for more than three-quarters of the
emerging-market total.
Emerging economies also generated most of the
growth in the global middle class. Taken together,

they accounted for more than 90% of the increase in
the middle and high-wealth classes over the past two
decades. The global wealth landscape has therefore
become far more diverse, even though the gap in
average wealth between advanced and emerging
markets remains wide.
The global low-wealth class grew by around 7% to
4.3bn people, while the population covered by the
analysis increased by almost 17%. Its share of the total
population consequently fell from 81.7% in 2005 to

74.8% in 2025. Emerging markets still accounted for
92.0% of this group, slightly below their 92.9% share two
decades earlier. However, the number of low-wealth
individuals in advanced economies increased by around
21% to 346mn. The global shift towards higher wealth
classes is therefore unmistakable, but it has by no means
benefited everyone.
The emergence of a larger and more geographically
diverse high-wealth class does not change the fact that

financial wealth remains extremely concentrated at the
global level. This becomes clear when the population of
the countries covered by the report is divided into deciles
according to net financial assets.
In 2025, the richest 10%, with average net financial
assets of approximately EUR312,590, owned 85.4% of
global net financial wealth. Their share has declined
from 91.5% in 2005, but remains far above the average
national top-decile share of 60.9% (Figure 30). At the

pace of improvement recorded over the past two
decades, it would take almost another eight decades
for global concentration to approach the level currently
observed within the countries.
13  The classification in wealth classes is based on worldwide average net financial assets per capita, which stood at EUR36,690 in 2025. The global
wealth middle class („middle wealth“, MW) includes all individuals with net financial assets of between 30% and 180% of the global average. This

means that for 2025, the rounded asset thresholds for the global wealth middle class are EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. The „low wealth“ (LW) catego -
ry, on the other hand, includes those individuals with net financial assets that are below a EUR11,000 threshold, while the term „high wealth“ (HW)
applies to those with net financial assets of more than EUR66,000 (for details on how the asset thresholds are set, see Appendix A).

### Page 38

Allianz Research
38
High global wealth concentration is also reflected in the
wide gap between median and average net financial
assets. In 2025, global median net financial wealth
stood at approximately EUR2,490 per capita, while the
average was almost 15 times as high at EUR36,690. The
gap, however, has narrowed substantially over time: in
2005, the average was almost 33 times the median. The
typical person has therefore gained ground relative to

the global average, but the average remains a poor
representation of ordinary wealth.
At first glance, the much faster growth of average net
wealth in most lower deciles appears encouraging
(Figure 31). However, these high growth rates largely
reflect extremely low starting points. Even large
percentage increases translate into modest absolute
gains, while the richest groups continue to accumulate
much larger sums. A more equal global distribution of

wealth therefore remains a distant prospect.

The gap between the richest decile and everyone else is

enormous. The second-richest decile owned just 8.7% of
global net financial assets, with an average of around
EUR34,060 per person. The richest tenth therefore held
nine times as much wealth per person as the group
immediately below it.
The bottom half of the population covered by the
report, comprising almost 3bn people, held virtually
nothing. Its combined share of global net financial
assets was approximately zero. The bottom decile had
negative net financial assets of around EUR2,680 per

person, as liabilities exceeded financial assets. Even
in the two deciles around the middle of the global
distribution, average net financial assets amounted to
only approximately EUR3,170 and EUR1,810 per person.
Global wealth has become more geographically diverse,
but it has hardly become broadly distributed.

39
National: More wealth, same divide
Net financial wealth is distributed less unevenly within
countries than across the world. In 2025, the richest 10%
owned an average of 60.9% of national net financial
assets across the 57 countries examined, using an
unweighted average, compared with 85.4% at the global
level. The gap between average and median wealth
was also considerably smaller: the national average-
to-median ratio was 3.12, again measured as an

unweighted average.
When it comes to inequality, however, the national
picture is particularly disappointing. Despite being a
major political issue for years, there has been virtually
no progress towards more even wealth distribution.
In 2005, the richest 10% held an average of 60.4% of
national wealth, while the average-to-median ratio
stood at 3.11. Two decades later, both measures are
almost unchanged. Wealth has grown substantially, but
its distribution within individual countries has remained

remarkably resistant to change.
This overall stability conceals sharper shifts at the
country level. In 32 of the 57 countries examined, the
share of the richest 10% changed by less than 2pps
between 2005 and 2025 (Figure 32). Where larger
shifts occurred, however, the balance was clearly tilted
towards greater inequality, with deterioration almost
twice as likely as improvement. Wealth concentration
increased in 16 countries and declined in only nine.

Among those recording an improvement, Cambodia
saw the largest decline in the top-decile share, followed
by Portugal and Slovakia. Latvia, Hungary, Belgium,
Ireland, Malaysia and the Netherlands also registered
meaningful decreases. Different starting points were at
work. Portugal, Latvia and Ireland moved down from
relatively high levels of concentration, while Slovakia,
Hungary and the Netherlands improved from already
more balanced positions. In the Netherlands, the richest

10% saw their share fall by 2.4pps to 56.1%.
At the other end of the spectrum, China recorded by far
the largest increase in concentration. The share of the
richest 10% rose by 15.7pps between 2005 and 2025 to
68.0%, well above the average national share of 60.9%.
This accompanied the enormous economic and social
transformation of the past two decades, which created
not only broad-based wealth but also a substantial
private upper class. China illustrates the difficulty of

combining rapid wealth creation with an equally rapid
diffusion of its benefits. After all, it was the unbridled
growth of the „wild“ 2000s, when the private sector
was subject to few restrictions, that primarily caused
inequality to rise. In sharp contrast, in the last five years,
the concentration of wealth has remained unchanged.

The ratio of average to median wealth provides an
important cross-check: it shows whether rising average
wealth is also reflected in the position of the typical

household. Across the 57 countries examined, the
average ratio remained virtually unchanged, rising from
3.11 in 2005 to 3.12 in 2025. This confirms the broader
picture of persistent national wealth inequality.
The strongest improvements occurred in the
Netherlands, where the ratio fell from 4.04 to 2.48, and
Ireland, where it declined from 4.87 to 3.42. In both
countries, the narrowing gap indicates that the middle
of the distribution gained ground relative to average

wealth.
The largest deteriorations were recorded in Russia,
Sweden and China. Russia’s ratio rose from 2.99 to 4.77,
while Sweden’s increased from 3.77 to 5.25. In China, it
climbed from 2.03 to 3.33. Median Chinese net financial
wealth increased substantially, but average wealth grew
faster. China thus illustrates a central tension of rapid
wealth creation: prosperity can spread while inequality
rises at the same time.
More broadly, the limited movement in both distribution

measures across most countries suggests that wealth
inequality is deeply entrenched. Two decades of
economic growth, crises and political attention have
barely altered the underlying distribution.

China was not alone. The top-decile share increased by
10.5pps in Russia and by 6.8pps in India, where rising

Concentration also rose in several advanced economies.

markets and with no improvement in sight. Sweden and
Switzerland also recorded increases of more than 4pps.
France saw a smaller rise of 3.1pps, although its top-
decile share of 54.8% remained below Germany’s 59.5%.
Rising concentration is therefore not confined to emerging
economies, nor does rapid development inevitably produce
it.

Appendix A: Methodological comments
General assumptions
The Allianz Global Wealth Report analyses gross financial assets held by households, i.e. cash and bank deposits,
receivables from insurance companies and pension institutions, securities (shares, bonds and investment funds) and other
receivables, as well as household liabilities. Net financial assets are defined as gross financial assets minus liabilities.
Real estate is excluded from this measure and analyzed separately where data are available. The report is based

on data from 57 countries. This group of countries covers 91% of global GDP and 72% of the global population. In 43
countries, we had access to statistics from the macroeconomic financial accounts. In the other countries, we were able to
estimate the volume of total financial assets based on information from household surveys, bank statistics, statistics on
assets held in equities and bonds and technical reserves.
To eliminate exchange-rate distortions over time, financial assets and liabilities denominated in national currencies are

converted into euros using exchange rates at the end of 2025. Unless explicitly described as real, these values are not
adjusted for inflation. The closing date for data to be included in the report is 31 July.
Statistical distinctions
The process associated with the introduction of the European System of Accounts 2010 (ESA 2010) in September 2014
involved updating and harmonizing the guidelines governing the preparation of many macroeconomic statistics. The

new requirements also apply to the macroeconomic financial accounts. One change relates to private households: under
the ESVG 2010 regulations, the two sectors „Private households“ and „Non-profit institutions serving households“ are
now reported separately. This also has implications for the Allianz Global Wealth Report, which takes data from the
macroeconomic financial accounts as a basis where available. For many countries, however – particularly those outside

of the EU – there is no separate data available for these sectors in general. So in order to ensure global comparability,
this publication analyzes both sectors together under the heading „private households“.
Determination of wealth bands for global wealth classes
Lower wealth threshold: There is a close link between financial assets and the incomes of private households. According
to Davies et al. (2009) , private individuals with below-average income tend to have no assets at all, or only very few. It is

only when individuals move into middle and higher income groups that they start to accumulate substantial assets.
We have applied this link to our analysis. Countries in the upper-middle income bracket (based on the World Bank‘s
country classification system) therefore form the group in which the average household assets first reach a meaningful
level. This value marks the lower threshold for the global middle wealth class.
In terms of income, households with incomes that correspond to between 75% and 150% of average net income are

generally considered to constitute the middle class. According to Davies et al., households with income corresponding
to 75% of the average income have assets that correspond to 30% of the average assets. As far as the upper threshold
is concerned, 150% of average income corresponds to 180% of average assets. Consequently, we have set the threshold
values for the wealth middle class at 30% and 180% of average net financial assets per capital. In 2025, average net

financial assets per capita in the countries covered stood at approximately EUR36,690. Applying these percentages gives
the rounded thresholds used in the workbook: EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. Individuals with net financial assets between
these thresholds belong to the global middle-wealth class. Those below the lower threshold belong to the low-wealth
class, while those above the upper threshold belong to the high-wealth class.
Appendices
14  Davies, James B. et al. (2009), The level and distribution of global household wealth, NBER working paper 15508.

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook
September 14, 2026
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.
IN THIS ISSUE
Macro Strategy—Superintelligence and Superabundance in the Next Decade:
Rapid advances in Artificial Intelligence (AI) have prompted prominent architects of
the new economy to conclude the Singularity is arriving ahead of schedule as signs
of its effects proliferate in the multitude of new AI models running in the increasing

number of data centers driving one of the biggest investment booms in U.S. history.
Superintelligence that surpasses collective human intelligence has long been one of the
predictions of futurists analyzing the implications of the Singularity. Superabundance is
one of the results of Superintelligence predicted by those who have written about the
consequences of this major acceleration in technological innovation. The early arrival of
these radical forces for change poses imminent challenges for society and investors that

will shape the next decade in critical ways.
In particular, the heightened uncertainty about how this new era plays out increases the
utility of a well-diversified approach to wealth management in our view.
Market View—Elevated Debt Supply Is Testing the Market's Appetite: The recent
rise in Treasury yields reflects a confluence of factors, including Federal Reserve (Fed)
policy uncertainty, deteriorating fiscal dynamics, resilient economic growth, persistent

inflation concerns and a record wave of debt issuance from both the U.S. Treasury and
corporate borrowers, particularly in the long end of the curve. Importantly, we view this
as a period of market adjustment rather than a cause for alarm.
Episodes of debt pressures can be a natural consequence of elevated supply, in addition
to other external factors and could be resolved through higher yields that help balance
supply and demand. While interest rate volatility may remain elevated and long-term

yields could settle at structurally higher levels, today's combination of attractive nominal
and real yields presents a potential opportunity for investors to extend beyond cash and
short-duration holdings and move closer to strategic duration targets.
The Ticker Tape—Top Questions Answered Directly by Our Chief Investment
Officer (CIO).
• What are the markets actually telling us?

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Superintelligence and Superabundance in the Next Decade
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
While some academics have claimed the Singularity is still off in the distant future, or even
unlikely at all, those on the front lines of AI model development have recently asserted that it
has already begun. For example, Elon Musk has pointed to recent issues with AI models going

outside their proscribed limits, including hacking external systems and completing previously
unsolved math problems. Over a year ago, Sam Altman wrote, "We are past the event horizon; the
takeoff has started. Humanity is close to building superintelligence..." In short, the rapid evolution
of these models has convinced knowledgeable observers that the Singularity has begun.
Superintelligence is the primary driver of the Singularity as exponential advances in AI ultimately

create an artificial Superintelligence that is much smarter and faster than the collective
intelligence of humanity. As such, it can solve practical problems that remain unsolved by humans
and do it in a timely fashion. Superabundance is the logical implication of Superintelligence
as it works to create a world where economic goods, services and energy are essentially free
because of the omniscience implied by the most efficient processing of unlimited information by

superintelligent models. In essence, Superintelligence could mimic the Creation.
Some aspects of Superabundance include automated production of desired output that
eliminates most physical labor, clean energy and synthetic materials that eliminate resource
scarcity, and increased longevity as Superintelligence is applied to maintaining physical health
via, for example, synthetic organ replacement and personalized illness treatments. Obviously,
Superabundance pulls the rug out from under traditional economics, which is the study of how

scarce resources are allocated. While the "scarcity" vanishes in this new world, the allocation issue
remains and is likely to become the focus of political debate. In particular, the question of how
jobs and incomes fit into a superabundant world is likely to become a bigger and bigger issue.
The trend of technology replacing more and more of the labor share of income is already decades
old and dates back to the beginnings of digital technology. That trend has picked up speed

with the increasing profit share of output the new technologies created. The ratio of income
to the number of workers is orders of magnitude higher for the big new technology companies
compared to the old economy companies of the past. This means the owners of capital
(shareholders) are receiving a bigger share of the economic pie as high-margin, high-productivity
companies based in the new technology become a bigger part of the economy. This trend is

accelerating. That is a direct implication of the movement toward and through the Singularity.
It means the issue of income inequality, which has always been on society's radar screen, will
become bigger in the years ahead unless there are wise policies to share the superabundance
of the near future. Some countries are better prepared for this future than others. Norway,
for example, has built a sovereign wealth fund (SWF) over the past 25 years that represents a

substantial share of capital income for each of its citizens. In the U.S., with its widespread equity
culture, thanks to Individual Retirement Accounts and 401K plans, capital income is more widely
distributed compared to Europe with its much more debt-based capital markets. In any event,
widespread equity ownership is one way to deal with a superabundant economy where AI has
significantly reduced the labor share of income.
Demographics add to the natural evolution from labor income to capital income as societies

become wealthier. Aging societies of retirees by definition depend on capital income more than
labor income. This transition of the economy to higher margins and faster earnings growth is
the fortunate accompaniment to the need for more income from capital as societies become
older. The longevity trend naturally accelerates this process, which is the logical result of
technological progress raising productivity and living standards. People live longer in wealthy

societies, as subsistence constraints on life spans diminish while lifestyle and mental health
constraints become more the issue. How lifestyles and mental health evolve in a world where
work becomes less necessary is already showing signs of their increased importance for the
future of public health.
The current accelerating profits cycle reflects this impact of new technology on the relative
returns to labor and capital. The trend so far has been relatively slow compared to what's

coming if Superintelligence and Superabundance are the outcomes the Singularity promises as
increasingly seems likely. Because much financial analysis relies on past cycles the accelerating
Investment Implications
Rapid innovation, political upheaval
and increasing creative destruction
raise the difficulty of valuing
longer duration assets as we saw
earlier this year in the software
space. Higher-risk premia are a
likely result. On balance, this favors

Value-oriented assets over Growth.
Higher real yields in a strong capital
spending environment reinforce this
preference.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
profits and rising margins of the current economy have naturally focused attention on what
happens when conditions revert to the mean of lower margins and more historically normal
profit growth. Looked at through this lens, Equities appear overvalued and destined to correct
bubble-like conditions. The Singularity, however, implies "this time is different," the four most
dangerous words in investing. Yet, the slowly evolving shift toward capital income already under

way is an early warning sign of bigger things to come as these trends are turbo-charged to warp
speed while the economy passes through the Singularity. Seen this way, a super-cycle in profits,
margins and stock values seems logical.
A revamping of the social safety net to address the conditions implied by Superabundance
is already a hot topic in Silicon Valley. Various proposals for a universal basic income (UBI)
are being bandied about. Typically, UBI refers to the government giving everyone an income

sufficient to meet some living standard. This automatically implies a big role for government in
the new economy. Given the fourth-turning breakdown in the political process and governance,
alternatives that avoid the government sector might also be considered. For example, another
implication of the Singularity is an accelerating accumulation of wealth fueling a growing supply
of altruistic and philanthropic potential that arguably could be administered more efficiently by

the private sector than the government where widespread allegations of corrupt administration
at all levels have proliferated since the pandemic. Given the growing wealth pie and the diversity
of social priorities among its owners addressing more social needs through philanthropy is a
likely option for the superabundant economy. In any event, the relative roles of government and
the private sector are necessarily an issue to be hashed out in this revolutionary time.

More glimmers of the UBI-like movements have popped up recently. As mentioned, Norway's
SWF is an example of how a well-administered government has been preparing for this future.
In the U.S. government sponsored tax-advantaged savings accounts from birth have been
instituted with private donations being added to the program. Governments, including the U.S.,
have increasingly been buying stakes in private investments for various purposes with the result

that the focus solely on sovereign debt can be a misleading indicator of fiscal fragility.
Japan is a significant example of the difference between gross government debt and net
government debt after subtracting government owned assets. Its gross debt is around 279% of
its gross domestic product (GDP) which is much higher than any other major economy. However,
Japan government assets total around 192% of GDP , including domestic and foreign Equities,

bonds, and loans. As a result, its net debt is less than 100% of GDP and more in line with other
major economies. As one of the most aged societies in the world, Japan points the way toward
how societies can cope with longevity. Government ownership of a broad array of higher yielding
global assets funded with low sovereign debt interest rates appears to be a path the U.S.
Treasury is also starting to pursue in managing its debt burden.
The main risks to the uptopian promise of Superabundance are the dystopian possibilities

that would follow from poor social decision-making. Around the world, different governments
will follow different approaches to navigating the unprecedented challenges posed by the
Singularity. As the U.S. celebrates its 250-year anniversary, it is in the midst of its fourth "fourth
turning." These are the intense political upheaval periods that demographers and historians have
documented in every fourth generation. The Revolutionary War, the Civil War, and the Great

Depression and World War II mark the first three "fourth turnings." The Singularity is a major
marker for the current fourth turning.
The relative success of the U.S. in transitioning through the previous major turning points in
history provides hope for a utopian rather than a dystopian outcome. Other countries have not
transited these challenges so well for such an extended period which helps explain how the
U.S. has become the most prosperous country on earth. Nevertheless, past performance is no

guarantee of future success. As always, each generation faces new challenges to overcome, or not.
For investors, the Singularity seems to be behind an unusually strong earnings cycle that could
potentially last much longer than past history would suggest. That's positive for Equities and
risk assets in general. The extra risks associated with this rapid transformation argue for well
diversified holdings as creative destruction on steroids seems a plausible outcome in a world of

warp speed change.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Elevated Debt Supply Is Testing the Market's Appetite
Chief Investment Office, Fixed Income Strategy Team
For much of the past two decades, rising debt levels appeared to carry few
consequences. Federal deficits expanded and corporate borrowing increased, yet
Treasury yields generally trended lower and credit spreads stayed remarkably tame.
Today, the backdrop may be changing.

Federal borrowing needs continue to grow as deficit spending remains elevated. Net
interest expense has surpassed $1 trillion annually and now rivals total U.S. defense
spending. On this front there appears no end in sight as the Congressional Budget
Office projects that the fiscal 2026 shortfall will widen to roughly $2.1 trillion. Investors
are increasingly focused on not only the level of debt outstanding, but also the pace at
which new debt is being issued—with some questioning about the sustainability of this

situation over the longer term.
This phenomenon is not limited to Treasurys, as the issuance glut extends to corporate
debt. Investment-grade (IG) corporate issuance is running roughly 40% above last year
and is on pace for a record year. 1 While demand for Fixed Income remains healthy, the
sheer volume of new debt being brought to market is beginning to test investor capacity,
particularly at the long end of the Treasury curve and within certain sectors.

Exhibit 1: Low Yields Post-2008/2009 GFC* Have Fueled a Borrowing Binge.

through 2026; 2026 reflects the first quarter.

Hyperscalers Are Competing for Long-Duration Capital. A significant driver of
the surge in corporate issuance has been the hyperscalers. The largest technology
companies are financing an unprecedented wave of AI-related capital spending, leading
to nearly $280 billion of debt issuance year-to-date. 2 Many of these issuers carry high
credit ratings and have ramped up issuance of longer-maturity bonds. As a result, they
are increasingly competing with long-dated Treasurys for the same pool of institutional

capital, including insurance companies, pension funds, sovereign wealth funds and
duration-oriented Fixed Income investors—creating a crowding-out effect amongst high-
quality Fixed Income.
Fortunately, this is a case of pressure, not a credit crisis. Recent weakness in long-dated
Treasurys reflects a classic supply-and-demand imbalance and importantly is not a
solvency crisis for the U.S. government or high-quality hyperscalers. Investors are being

asked to absorb a growing volume of net new issuance from both the U.S. Treasury and
corporate America at the same time.
Debt Pressure and a Normalization, Not a Fiscal Reckoning. The rise in long-end
yields during August reflects this dynamic. The 30-year Treasury yield moved above
5.3%, reaching levels not seen since 2007. Fiscal concerns likely played a role, but we
1 Source: Bloomberg as of September 8, 2026.
2 Source: Bloomberg as of September 9, 2026.

Investment Implications
The recent rise in Treasury yields
reflects a collision of fiscal co ncerns,
resilient growth, persistent inflation,
and record debt issuance from both
the Treasury and corporate borrowers.
While this has created periodic b outs
of market pressures, we view the
recent move as a normalization
process, not a cri sis of confidence .
Attractive real and nominal yield s
reinforce our vie w that investors
should look beyond cash and short-

duration assets and move closer to
strategic durati on target.

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
believe heavy supply, sticky inflation, reduced Fed demand and increased competition for
investor capital were equally important contributors.
And importantly, the market's adjustment mechanism is already working. Higher Treasury
yields and somewhat wider credit spreads are attracting buyers and helping clear the growing
supply of bonds. This is a natural normalization and is how markets restore equilibrium.

Recent Treasury auctions continue to attract demand consistent with historical norms, and
investor appetite for new IG issuance has remained robust. These are not characteristics
typically associated with a loss of confidence in U.S., or corporate, creditworthiness.
A New Yield Regime. For Fixed Income investors, the more important implication
is that a 4% to 5% 10-year Treasury yield may represent the new normal. In an
environment of very strong underlying economic growth and unprecedented volumes of

debt issuance from both the public and private sectors, higher yields may be required to
attract sufficient capital amid a more competitive landscape for yield.
We continue to view both nominal and real yields as attractive and believe investors
should remain focused on achieving strategic duration targets. While rate volatility may
persist, today's yield levels provide a potential opportunity to seek attractive income that
has been largely absent for much of the past decade.

The Bigger Picture: Growth Still Matters More Than Debt Levels. None of this dismisses
the long-term fiscal challenges facing the U.S. Debt levels are rising, interest costs
continue to grow, and mandatory spending increasingly consumes a larger share of the
federal budget. However, history shows that debt levels alone do not determine interest
rates. Between 2007 and 2020, U.S. debt rose dramatically even as the 10-year Treasury
yield fell from roughly 5% to below 1%. What ultimately matters is the interplay between

short-term interest rates, debt levels, economic growth, inflation and investor demand.
For instance, nominal GDP growth and the 10-year yield tend to be correlated, outside
of recessions—we believe recent rate increases partly reflect this strong macro
backdrop. Moreover, economic growth is running above long-end yields, supporting the
sustainability of rising government debt.

Reserve Board; National Bureau of Economic Research. Data as of September 9, 2026.
The U.S. continues to benefit from several structural advantages: the dollar remains
the world's reserve currency, Treasurys remain the foundation of the global financial

system, and U.S. capital markets are still the deepest and most liquid in the world. Those
advantages continue to support demand for U.S. assets and underpin sovereign credit
quality even as borrowing needs grow.
The bottom line, in our view, is that even though the recent rise in long-end yields catches
many headlines, there is no need to panic. Instead, the recent repricing reflects periodic
episodes of debt pressure rather than a fundamental loss of confidence in U.S. credit. With

borrowing demand from both Washington and corporate America likely to remain elevated,
bond prices may continue to serve as the market's release valve. The consequence is likely
a world of structurally higher long-term yields, not a sovereign debt crisis.

### Page 6

6 of 8 September 14, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What are the markets actually telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investmen t Officer
When the markets speak loudly we should listen. There are often clues in the internal trends
within the markets that help us determine how far into an economic cycle we are or what drivers
of growth are most impactful. In addition, we can also find clues that help with determining

the micro picture. In other words, what industries are the most likely to benefit, from a
profitability standpoint is concerned, as the growth picture unfolds? Currently, the markets have
a megaphone, in our view. The year-to-date sharp outperformers have been consistent and
should continue to advance. These include commodities, including copper and oil, Emerging
Markets, Japan in the Developed Markets, Small-cap relative to Large-cap shares, Value versus

Growth, and more cyclically-oriented companies versus more defensive. This current consistent
outperformance informs us that we are still early- to mid-cycle in a supply-led expansion. We are
still early in the build out of the supply that is needed for the natural demand that is there. New
infrastructure is needed to handle the new economy that is progressing and quickly innovating.
This infrastructure needs commodities, all forms of energy, battery storage, minerals, metals,

recycled water, gases, chemicals, and all the compute and cloud capacity, including memory
and other components to handle the path toward mass tokenization. Moreover, the buildup of
government debt in the advanced economies as fiscal deficits widen is forcing large institutional
investors (including central banks and other government entities) to diversify their reserve
bases. This has increased the interest in gold holdings. Countries that have a vast amount of

natural resources, especially oil and gas, are in high demand by investors, which is obvious when
examining year-to-date outperformers. The bottom line is that the markets are telling us that we
are still early- to mid-cycle in this massive buildout, which will require a wide collection of hard
assets, Infrastructure (old and new), land, and within Equities, a more diversified approach to
exposure—particularly areas that are more cyclical- and Value-oriented. We expect this view to

extend itself well into the foreseeable future if not years.
In Fixed Income, it may seem more complex, but it is actually easier to understand what the
markets are telling us, in our opinion. The backup in
yields is “real yield” driven, which is more due
to higher nominal growth than to concerns of long-run inflation. This is evident in the Treasury
inflation protection market, break-evens in general and the ratio of Treasury yields relative to
other Go

vernment bond markets in the developed world. However, rising oil and gas prices and
inflation gauges that are still above the preferred level are usually the excuses for yields backing
up. Deficit concerns are a more plausible reason f
or yields rising but the main driver, in our view,
is still abo
ve average nominal growth. The yield curve and rates in general are still adjusting to
this new economic landscape that is very different than those of the most recent past.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Bloomberg Commodity Index designed to provide liquid and diversified exposure to commodities via futures contracts. Is calculated on an excess return basis and reflects commodity
futures price movements.
MSCI Emerging Markets Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets countries. With 1,205 constituents, the index covers approximately 85% of the free
float adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI Japan Index is designed to measure the performance of the large and mid cap segments of the Japanese market.

MSCI Pacific ex Japan Index captures large and mid cap representation across 4 of 5 Developed Markets (DM) countries in the Pacific region (excluding Japan). With 93 constituents, the index
covers approximately 85% of the free float-adjusted market capitalization in each country.
MSCI All-Country World Index is a premier benchmark representing global equity performance, covering large and mid-cap stocks across 23 developed and 24 emerging markets.

MSCI World Index ex U.S. captures large and mid cap representation across 22 of 23 Developed Markets countries--excluding the United States..
MSCI USA Momentum Index is based on MSCI USA Index, its parent index, which captures large and mid-cap stocks of the U.S. market. It is designed to reflect the performance of an equity
momentum strategy by emphasizing stocks with high price momentum, while maintaining reasonably high trading liquidity, investment capacity and moderate index turnover.

U.S. Large-cap Growth/Russell 1000 Growth Index measures the performance of the large-cap growth segment of the U.S. equity universe. It includes those Russell 1000 companies with
relatively higher price-to-book ratios, higher I/B/E/S forecast medium term (2 year) growth and higher sales per share historical growth (5 years).
U.S. Small-cap/Russell 2000 Index measures the performance of the small-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe.
MSCI USA High Dividend Yield Index is designed to reflect the performance of equities in the MSCI USA Index (excluding REIT s) with higher dividend income and quality characteristics than

average dividend yields that are both sustainable and persistent.
U.S. Mid Cap/Russell Midcap Index measures the performance of the mid-cap segment of the U.S. Equity universe. The Russell Midcap Index is a subset of the Russell 1000® Index. It
includes approximately 800 of the smallest securities based on a combination of their market cap and current index membership.
MSCI USA Value Index captures large and mid-cap U.S. securities exhibiting overall value style characteristics.

MSCI USA Equal Weighted Index represents an alternative weighting scheme to its market cap weighted parent index, the MSCI USA Index. At each quarterly rebalance date, all index
constituents are weighted equally, effectively removing the influence of each constituent’s current price.
MSCI USA Minimum Volatility Index aims to reflect the performance characteristics of a minimum variance strategy applied to the large and mid-cap USA equity universe.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

## Document 315: Eurozone _ Growth on track despite the turbulence (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Eurozone _ Growth on track despite the turbulence (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
GROWTH ON TRACK DESPITE THE TURBULENCE
Growth was stronger than expected in Q2 and is set to strengthen further in the coming quarters. According to our fore-
casts, it will reach 1.5% in 2027 after 1.1% in 2026. This growth is expected to be driven by investment and its knock-on
effect on exports. On an annual average basis, inflation is expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and 2.8% in 2027, peaking at

the end of 2026. The ECB is expected to continue its monetary tightening. At this stage, a single further rate rise, aimed at
keeping inflation expectations anchored, is expected in December. The impact of rising interest rates (key policy rates and
market rates) on public finances and private sector borrowing conditions will be worth monitoring.
GROWTH: BEYOND THE SHOCKS, A RESILIENT MOMENTUM
Growth in the Eurozone remained robust in the first half of 2026, at

0.25% q/q in Q1 and 0.33% q/q in Q2, excluding Ireland (where economic
activity is volatile). It was driven by Germany, where signs of a recovery
are becoming clearer (see Germany). Our baseline scenario forecasts
growth of 1.1% in 2026, followed by 1.5% in 2027 on an annual average
basis. This growth is expected to be driven by the strengthening of
trends already underway in public and private investment (particularly
in defence and AI) and in exports (trade in services and intra-zone trade

in goods). Household consumption is likely to be resilient too. According
to our forecasts, job creation would continue to underpin household
incomes, thereby cushioning the impact of inflation.
A NEW LEASE OF LIFE FOR INDUSTRY
The Eurozone is undergoing one of the most profound industrial trans-
formations in its history. This is giving rise to divergences between key
sectors but, above all, to new drivers of growth whose effects are beco-
ming increasingly apparent (see our analysis). Indeed, the signs of an

economic recovery are growing stronger. Business sentiment indicators
show a marked improvement in the economic outlook across most sec-
tors (Chart 2), driven primarily by electronics (AI, digitalisation), the
automotive sector and aerospace. European industry is thus benefi -
ting from strong demand for capital goods; a structural trend that will
continue to be fuelled by ongoing investment cycles in AI, defence and
electrification. The introduction of minimum European content quotas

in the draft law on the industrial accelerator, together with the Com -
mission’s intention to tighten the criteria for access to European public
procurement markets, will provide additional support for European in-
dustry in the longer term.
FOREIGN TRADE: A REPOSITIONING THAT IS PAYING OFF
Following a decline in 2026, linked to rising energy import costs, the
trade surplus is expected to rise to around 3.0% of GDP in 2027 (of
which 2% of GDP for goods and 1% for services). Growth in European ex-

ports has slowed compared with its pre-Covid trend and is being ham-
pered by the decline in Chinese and US markets. However, Eurozone
exports in volume rose significantly in Q2 (+3.4% q/q).
More importantly, since Liberation Day (April 2025), Eurozone coun -
tries have found significant sources of growth amongst other European
partners, which has enabled them to cushion the decline in exports to
certain third markets. This is evident in exports to the rest of the EU,

EFTA countries (Switzerland in particular) and, to a lesser extent, the
United Kingdom, Ukraine and the Balkans (Chart 3). The momentum of
intra-European trade therefore remains very strong. It is expected to
strengthen further with the anticipated investments in defence, AI and
electrification.
FINANCING: FINANCIAL CONDITIONS ARE EXPECTED TO REMAIN TIGHT
Financial conditions in the Eurozone remained tight in Q2 2026, accor-
ding to indices calculated by the ECB (including the BIG [Broad Inter -

mediation Gauge]). They are expected to tighten more significantly in
the second half of the year: i/ the rise in long-term bond yields, which
has accelerated since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran, is expected to
continue; and ii/ the ECB will continue its monetary tightening (+25 bps

The bank
for a changing
world
in Q3, then in Q4). For the time being, the debt burden is still weighing
only moderately on public finances due to the long average maturity of
countries’ debt (8.2 years on average in the Eurozone in July), but this
burden will increase by 2030.
On the corporate front, the rise in interest rates has remained contained
(+2 bps since January 2026, reaching 3.9% in July), which has contri -

buted to the strong growth in new investment loans (cumulated over
one year, +9.1% y/y in July compared with 15.3% y/y in September 2025).
By contrast, cumulative net debt securities issues over one year re -
mained stable in July, whilst cumulative net issues of listed shares over
one year halved month on month.
Rising interest rates will continue to hamper new variable-rate
home loans to households (around 65% of new lending) more signi -
ficantly than fixed-rate loans, for which rates stood in July at 3.69%

(+4 bps m/m) and 3.36% (+2 bps m/m) respectively. New home loans,
which are stable in 2026 on a cumulative over one year basis, are there-
fore unlikely to see any improvement. A fall in real-estate transactions
could weigh on new consumer credit through a reduction in expenditure
on home furnishings and renovations, whereas consumer credit has
been fairly buoyant so far (year-to-date, +5.2% y/y in July compared
with +1.4% y/y in January).
LABOUR MARKET: RESILIENCE YET TO BE CONFIRMED

Employment in the Eurozone rose again by an average of 0.1% q/q in
the first half of 2026, driven by Spain and Portugal. Given the growth
outlook in these two countries, this momentum is likely to continue,
whilst business sentiment surveys in Germany suggest that employ -
ment is beginning to stabilise. Nevertheless, employment levels are
plateauing (France, the Netherlands) or even falling in other countries
(Austria, Finland), which is likely to slow the decline in the Eurozone

unemployment rate, meaning it is expected to remain close to current
levels in 2027. Furthermore, second-round effects of the rise in inflation
on wages cannot be ruled out: the ECB’s wage tracker suggests wage
growth of close to 3% by summer 2027 (2.9% y/y in June 2027, compared
with 2.6% in September).
INFLATION: A PEAK EXPECTED IN LATE 2026
Changes in wage dynamics will be a key factor in the inflation out -
look for 2027, whilst energy price rises are picking up again, driven

by the conflict in the Middle East. This rise is even sharper than in
the spring for gas (linked to restocking ahead of winter) and is likely
to feed through to electricity prices in several countries. We therefore
anticipate inflation peaking at 3.7% y/y in Q4 2026, before easing as the
energy component falls. On an annual average, headline inflation is
expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and then at 2.8% in 2027.
At this stage, the pass-through of rising energy prices to other com -

ponents of the consumer price index, particularly services, remains li -
mited. The alternative core indicators monitored by the ECB, including
the PCCI (Persistent and Common Component of Inflation, 2.1% in July),
remain close to the 2% target. Core inflation, however, is expected to
peak two months later than headline inflation (3% in March 2027).
Inflationary risks are nevertheless tilted to the upside. These are lin -
ked to food prices, which are expected to be affected by the energy

shock and weather conditions (summer drought, El Niño); they could
also stem from a delayed impact of energy price rises on underlying
inflation. The rise in prices of energy-dependent inputs (notably che -
micals and plastics) and those linked to the AI sector is expected to be
sustained. Growth, which we expect to remain robust over the coming
quarters, will also fuel underlying inflationary pressures.
MONETARY POLICY: TIGHTENING WITHOUT SLOWING (GROWTH)

Following two 25-basis-point rises (in June and September), we ex -
pect a third in December, which would bring the deposit facility rate
to 2.75%. It would then be in slightly restrictive territory (we estimate
the upper bound of the neutral rate to be 2.5%). This rise would be
aimed primarily at anchoring inflation expectations and, consequently,
at curbing the pass-through effects of inflation. Monetary tightening
would therefore remain limited. Indeed, the overshoot of the inflation

target would be temporary, and the operational framework adopted by
the ECB in June 2025 allows it to tolerate this, provided it remains brief.
However, it cannot be ruled out that the ECB might take further action
if the decline in inflation proves slower than expected.
PUBLIC FINANCES ARE CONTINGENT ON RISING INTEREST RATES
The fiscal impulse in the Eurozone is expected to be clearly positive in
2026 due to the rise in defence and infrastructure spending in Germany.

This will contribute to a widening of the country’s primary deficit (from
+0.9 pp of GDP to 2.8%, according to our forecasts). The improvement in
public finances is expected to stall in both France and Italy, at least in
2026, due to the impact of inflation on the debt burden via index-linked
bonds. This trend is expected to reverse in 2027 with less expansionary
fiscal policies. Nevertheless, the increase in the debt service burden
would fuel a further rise in the public deficit, which would thus reach

around 3.3% and 3.5% of GDP in 2026 and 2027 respectively (compared
with 2.9% in 2025). Against this backdrop, the Eurozone’s public debt-
to-GDP ratio is projected to exceed 90% of GDP in 2026 and reach 93%
of GDP in 2027, according to our forecasts.
Article completed on 15 September 2026

## Document 359: Meta Q3营收与Q4指引双超预期，上调AI资本开支下限，拟发行无投票权新股｜财报见闻

Source PDF: all report pdf/Meta Q3营收与Q4指引双超预期，上调AI资本开支下限，拟发行无投票权新股｜财报见闻.pdf

Meta Q3 营收 646 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 26% ，预计 Q4 营收 730 亿⾄ 760 亿美元；⾃由现⾦流负
22 亿美元，⾃上市以来⾸次转负，但好于市场预期的负 94 亿美元。公司将 2026 年资本开⽀
指引下限从此前的 1300 亿美元上调⾄ 1350 亿美元，同时宣布创设新的⽆投票权 C 类普通
股，换取扎克伯格承诺留居加州。 Meta 盘后⼀度上涨 6% 。
摘要：
Meta 第三季度营收创历史新⾼，同时宣布将发⾏⽆投票权新类别股票，避免扎克伯格因加州财
富税离开公司总部所在辖区，同时为规模庞⼤的⼈⼯智能投资计划开辟融资渠道。
Meta 周三公布，第三季度营收达 646 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 26% ，超出分析师预期的 633 亿美元。公
司预计第四季度营收将达到 730 亿⾄ 760 亿美元，区间中值⾼于市场预期。
利润表现则受到⼀次性法律和解费⽤的明显影响。 Meta 第三季度摊薄后每股收益为 3.83 美元，
低于分析师预期的约 6.54 美元。公司计⼊ 98.9 亿美元法律和解费⽤，剔除该费⽤后，每股收益为
7.40 美元。

与此同时，Meta 将 2026 年资本开⽀指引下限从此前的 1300 亿美元上调⾄ 1350 亿美元，上限维
持 1450 亿美元不变。
Meta 盘后⼀度上涨 6% ，后有所回落，截⾄发稿上涨 2.2% 。
⾃由现⾦流转负仍上调资本开⽀下限，新股融资⽀撑 AI 军备竞赛
Meta ⾃由现⾦流⾃上市以来⾸次转负，报告期内为负 22 亿美元，但好于分析师此前预期的负 94
亿美元。

### Page 2

公司正将数百亿美元投⼊芯⽚和数据中⼼建设，以期在与 OpenAI 、 Anthropic 的竞争中占据更有
利位置，这⼀投⼊持续消耗其历史上较为充裕的现⾦流。
第三季度， Meta 资本开⽀（包括融资租赁本⾦偿付）达到 349.1 亿美元。公司将全年资本开⽀指
引调整⾄ 1350 亿⾄ 1450 亿美元，此前下限为 1300 亿美元，反映出其仍在为 AI 基础设施扩张投资
⼤量资⾦。
公司宣布创设新的⽆投票权 C 类普通股，计划于 2027 年 1 ⽉⽣效。这⼀安排旨在为公司的 AI 投资
提供更多资⾦来源，同时避免削弱 CEO 扎克伯格的投票控制权。
同时，这种新类别的股票将通过股票股利的形式发放给现有投资者。 Meta 表⽰，持有⼈每持有
⼀股将获得⼀股新股，这实质上等同于⼀次 “ ⼀拆⼆ ” 拆股。
Meta 发⾏⽆投票权新股，换取扎克伯格承诺留居加州
Meta 与扎克伯格达成协议，将向其发⾏⽆投票权 C 类股票。作为交换，扎克伯格承诺未来⼋年
继续居住在加州或 Meta 总部所在的其他司法辖区。

这项安排也与加州拟议中的亿万富豪财富税有关。扎克伯格曾表⽰，相关税收可能迫使其出售
具有投票权的 B 类股票以筹集税款，使其在公司的投票权⽐例降⾄ 53% 以下。
根据协议，扎克伯格可以⽴即出售所获 C 类股票的 20% ，相当于其整体经济权益的 10% ，其余股
份的出售限制将在⼋年内逐步解除。
M e t a 特 别 委 员 会 称 ， 确 保 扎 克 伯 格 在 公 司 总 部 办 公 ⾄ 关 重 要 ， 尤 其 是 在 公 司 与 M e t a
Superintelligence Labs 密切合作、 AI 竞争⽇益激烈之际。
Meta ⾸席财务官 Susan Li 则表⽰， C 类股票主要作为融资⼯具，帮助公司更灵活地为 AI 业务筹
资，同时避免稀释现有股东的投票权。
⼴告业务保持增⻓，第四季度营收指引超预期
⼴告业务仍是 Meta 业绩增⻓的主要⽀撑。第三季度，公司⼴告展⽰量同⽐增⻓ 10% ，平均⼴告
价格上涨 14% 。家庭⽇活跃⼈数达到 36.4 亿，同⽐增⻓ 3% 。

Meta 预计第四季度营收为 730 亿⾄ 760 亿美元，区间中值为 745 亿美元，较上年同期增⻓约
24% 。该指引⾼于 LSEG 汇总的分析师平均预期 730 亿美元左右，显⽰公司对年末⼴告需求和业
务增⻓保持信⼼。
⼴告业务的增⻓也为 AI 投资提供了重要⽀撑。 Meta 正将 AI 应⽤于⼴告投放和定向等环节，同时
尝试通过新的 AI 产品拓展⼴告以外的收⼊来源。不过，新业务的商业化进展能否匹配基础设施投
⼊，仍有待后续业绩验证。
AI 助⼿ Muse 成商业化核⼼，但障碍犹存
Meta 将消费者 AI 助⼿ Muse 定位为整体战略的核⼼，并围绕负责该产品的 Meta 超级智能实验室对
公司架构进⾏重组，同时宣布推出⾯向企业客⼾销售 AI ⼯具的新业务线。
据市场情报机构 SensorTower 数据， Muse ⾃ 9 ⽉上线以来下载量已超过 1000 万次，该应⽤提供
每⽉ 20 美元和 100 美元两档订阅⽅案。

法律和解费⽤拖累利润，成本增⻓值得关注

解费⽤，导致报告利润与剔除⼀次性费⽤后的盈利⽔平出现明显差异。
第三季度营收⾼于市场预期，但报告每股收益低于分析师预期。剔除上述法律和解费⽤后，每

股收益达到 7.40 美元，超过市场预期。
今年 8 ⽉， Meta 与美国近 48 个州达成和解，同意在⼗年内⽀付最⾼ 180 亿美元，并承诺对未成年
⽤⼾使⽤ Facebook 和 Instagram 的⽅式作出⼀系列限制，指控称 Meta 平台被刻意设计以使⼉童
成瘾。
在本季度财报发布当天，新墨西哥州⼀名法官裁定 Meta ⽀付 250 亿美元罚款，陪审团认定 Meta
就其与第三⽅共享⽤⼾数据的⽅式及平台仇恨⾔论和虚假信息政策执⾏情况存在误导⾏为，该
案涉及英国政治咨询公司剑桥分析。
不过，费⽤压⼒并不完全来⾃单⼀法律事项。 Meta 第三季度总成本与费⽤同⽐增⻓ 68% ，达到
516.1 亿美元，明显快于 26% 的营收增幅。营业利润率由上年同期的 40% 降⾄ 20% ，反映出公司
在扩⼤ AI 投⼊的同时，盈利能⼒受到挤压。
Meta 预计 2026 年全年总费⽤将达到 1750 亿⾄ 1790 亿美元。随着 AI 相关⽀出继续增⻓，投资者需
要同时衡量⼴告业务的增⻓、成本扩张速度以及现⾦流变化，⽽不能仅凭剔除⼀次性费⽤后的

每股收益判断经营表现。

20 万颗英伟达 GB300 GPU ，⽬标 2027 年实现 13GW AI 算⼒、包括 44 万颗英伟达 Rubin

摘要：
SpaceX 三季度业绩迎来⼤爆发，⻢斯克不仅抛出 2027 年保底 13GW 、甚⾄挑战 20GW 的 AI 算⼒
狂暴扩张计划，更豪⾔公司年经常性收⼊将在明年底冲刺万亿美元⼤关。
华尔街⻅闻提及，美东时间 20 ⽇周⼆美股盘后， SpaceX 公布，截⾄ 9 ⽉的 2026 年第三季度，公
司营收同⽐增⻓ 161% ⾄ 145.8 亿美元，较分析师预期的约 128 亿美元⾼ 10% 以上。
营收暴增的背后，是超级智能云服务业务的爆发式增⻓。超级智能业务单季营收达 76 亿美元，
环⽐激增 50 亿美元，同⽐增加 67 亿美元。与此同时，净利润从去年同期亏损 11 亿美元转为盈利

44 亿美元，调整后 EBITDA 达到 88 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 306% 。

的回收周期已压缩⾄不⾜⼀年，且短期合同续签价格持续⾛⾼。
⻢斯克进⼀步预测公司2027 年年度经常性收⼊（ ARR ）有望达到 5000 亿⾄ 1 万亿美元区间。
⻢斯克称，到 2026 年底，将有 2.5 ⾄ 3GW 的算⼒投⼊运⾏，SpaceX 相当有信⼼在 2027 年实现
10GW 算⼒上线，说如果到明年年底上线算⼒不⾜ 13GW ，我会感到意外。
为⽀持上述扩张， SpaceX 计划 2027 年额外增加 20GW 的电⼒供应及冷却基础设施。⻢斯克称，
SpaceX 部署电⼒、冷却及配套基础设施的速度，有望超过其他所有⼚商的总和。
关于⽕箭发射和星链数据， SpaceX ⾸席运营官 Gwynne Shotwell 在电话会上表⽰，公司有望在
明年初实现⽕箭⼆级复⽤发射。 Shotwell 称，星链航空终端产量预计在今年年底前翻番。

Shotwell 透露，星链移动直连⼿机业务⽉活⽤⼾已突破 2200 万。收购频谱的交易将让星链成为
美国主要移动运营商。星链企业业务收⼊与政府业务收⼊未来均有望超过消费者业务收⼊。星
链有望承载全球互联⽹流量中相当⼤的⼀部分。

我们实际上被每⼀个超⼤规模云⼚商以及⼏⼗家⼩公司⽆情地围追堵截，要求租⽤我们更多的算

⼈ …… 我 们 试 图 把 ⼀ 些 推 理 算 ⼒ 外 包 给 其 他 云 服 务 商 ， 结 果 就 遭 遇 了 ‘ 奶 酪 店 场 景 ’ 。 我 们
问 ‘GB300 有吗？ ’ 没有。 ‘GB200 呢？ ’ 没有。 ‘TPU 呢？ ’ 没有。 ‘H100 呢？ ’ 也没有。这简直太荒谬
了。
简⽽⾔之，这意味着到 2027 年底， SpaceX 的年度经常性收⼊（ ARR ）将介于 5000 亿到 1 万亿美
元之间。
这是⽂明意识历史上⼀个意义深远的技术⾥程碑 …… 如果第 15 次⻜⾏没有成功，我们也终将实
现它。

当前， SpaceX 在发射、连接和超级智能三个领域都迎来了重要的拐点。
星舰⽅⾯，第 14 次⻜⾏是⼀个关键⾥程碑 ——Sasha ⾸次进⼊轨道，并成功部署了运营级星链
V3 卫星。我们有⼀段回顾视频稍后播放。第 15 次⻜⾏预计将在下个⽉进⾏，我们将尝试同时回
收助推器和⻜船。如果成功，这将是轨道运载⽕箭史上⾸次实现完全回收，是⼀个重⼤的技术⾥
程碑。
实现意识在地球之外的持续延伸，完全可重复使⽤的轨道运载⽕箭是唯⼀的途径。因此，我认为
这是⼈类⽂明史上⼀个意义深远的技术⾥程碑。接下来我们将播放⼀段第 15 次⻜⾏的模拟动
画，展⽰成功情形下的⻜⾏过程。
即便第 15 次⻜⾏未能成功，我们也会坚持下去。⾸⼏次⻜⾏的成功并⽆百分之百的保证，但我
认为下次⻜⾏成功的概率超过 50% 。如果没有成功，我们在今年年底前还将持续安排星舰⻜⾏尝
试，并在明年进⼀步提⾼频率。
我们还宣布了路易斯安那州航天发射场的开发计划。这将是⼀项对美国乃⾄全世界都极具价值的
重要资产，预计届时将建有约 20 座或更多发射塔，每年能够向轨道及更远空间运输数百万吨的

有效载荷，意义⾮凡。
我们预计，星舰最早将在明年上半年实现每周⼀⻜，明年下半年达到每周两⻜，甚⾄到明年年底
实现更⾼频率。随着快速完全可重复使⽤技术的突破，星舰的发射频率将⼤幅提升。
实现完全可重复使⽤后，最初每次⻜⾏之间还需要少量检修⼯作，但我们将逐步消除检修需求，
最终实现⽕箭落地后即可再次发射，就像⻜机⼀样。如我之前所说，这是⼀个意义深远的技术⾥
程碑，尽管⽬前⼤多数⼈尚未意识到其重要性，但回头来看，这将是显⽽易⻅的。
星链⽅⾯，同样刚刚起步。第 14 次⻜⾏部署了 26 Tbps （太⽐特每秒）的星链带宽，相当于 10 次
猎鹰 9 号发射的带宽总量。也就是说，仅⼀次星舰发射 —— 还只是半载 —— 就实现了相当于 10 次
猎鹰 9 号发射的效果。
我预计，不需要太久，⼤约三年之内，我们星链星座的带宽将达到当前的约 100 倍 —— 因为我们
将发射带宽提升⼀个数量级的卫星，同时将星链卫星总数也提升⼀个数量级，⼤约达到 10 万
颗，每颗卫星的带宽均超过现有星链系统的 10 倍。星链的营收将与所提供的总带宽⼤体成⽐例

增⻓。
我们还将具备⾼带宽的直连终端（ direct-to-cell ）能⼒，并将建设地⾯⽹络，在所有  SpaceX 与
星链获准运营的地区提供⼴泛连接。我认为，未来 3 到 5 年内，带宽需求将远超今⽇ —— 可能是
当前需求的 1000 倍。原因在于， AI 智能体在带宽消耗上将远超单个⼈类⽤⼾，⽽智能体的数量
也将远超⼈类数量，这就需要通信带宽实现数个数量级的提升，才能满⾜庞⼤的通信需求。
超级智能⽅⾯，我称之为  SpaceX SI （ SpaceX 超级智能）。⼈们正在逐渐意识到，制约超级智
能部署的瓶颈，更多在于电⼒、冷却和基础设施，⽽⾮芯⽚本⾝。
SpaceX 拥有⼀⽀卓越的团队，在地球质量⼊轨份额上已达到近 90% ，中国约占 6% ，其余国家
和地区（包括美国其他⼒量）合计约占 4% 。这⽀团队所取得的成绩，⽐所有太空竞争对⼿加在
⼀起还要⾼出⼀个数量级。如今，我们将这⽀同样的团队应⽤于地⾯算⼒的部署。这就好⽐纽约
洋基队去打⼩联盟，胜负悬念⼏乎为零。⽽最终的结果，就是我们能够以远超其他任何企业的速

度部署电⼒与冷却基础设施，甚⾄在某些时候可能超过所有其他公司的总和。请各位好好消化⼀
下这句话。
我个⼈最近花了⼤量时间在孟菲斯，经常⼀周去四天，⽇夜不停地推进孟菲斯超级计算集群上线
⼯作。我们计划今年剩余时间内每⽉稳定上线约 20 万块  GV300 ，明年将延续这⼀节奏，且明年

的主⼒将切换⾄  Vera Rubin 算⼒。⽬前，我对 2027 年能够再上线 10 GW 算⼒相当有把握，这

低频段频谱资产，为星链移动成为美国主要移动运营商奠定基础。
超级智能⽅⾯，⾏业对⼤规模算⼒的需求持续强劲。我们正在快速扩充算⼒，以满⾜  Grok 及相
关产品规模化的内部需求，以及云服务外部客⼾的需求。企业客⼾对近期模型发布的反馈积极，
Grok Bot 的市场采⽤势头尤为强劲。 Grok Bot （我们的智能体助⼿）于 8 ⽉上线，截⾄本⽉初已
拥有 80 万付费周活跃⽤⼾，较上⽉翻倍增⻓。 B2B 是增⻓最快的业务类别，越来越多的企业将

Grok Bot 作为销售、市场、 IT 和⼯程领域的效能倍增器，将其视为应对复杂端到端⼯作流的全
天候团队成员。
我们同时在加快  Grok 模型发布节奏，近期已实现显著性能提升，后续还将持续优化。与此同
时， Cursor 的整合⼯作进展顺利，进⼀步增强了我们的产品能⼒和规模化企业销售触达。这⼀
势头已有初步佐证：包含  Cursor 在内、年化运营收⼊超过 100 万美元的  Grok 企业客⼾数量，

### Page 8

以备考⼝径计算，较上季度增⻓ 50% 。上周，我们进⼀步整合产品组合，推出了全新⽅案，将
Grok 、 Grok Bot 、 Cursor 和  X Premium 整合为⼀个套餐。
综合来看，我们的业务在三⼤板块均呈现强劲发展势头。⼯程团队持续推进星舰和新⼀代卫星
（覆盖宽带、移动和  Starshield ）的规模化进程，商业团队在航空、企业、政府及全球市场持续
推动星链拓展。与此同时，我们凭借硬件专⻓，在关键算⼒基础设施领域加速扩张超级智能业
务。下⾯有请  Bret 发⾔。
⾸席财务官  Bret Johnsen：谢谢， Gwynne 。第三季度，我们再度交出亮眼成绩。
营收在连接业务持续强劲势头及超级智能（ SI ）业务明显拐点的推动下，同⽐⼤幅增⻓ 161% ，
达到 146 亿美元。 EBITDA 增速再度超越营收增速，达到 88 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 306% 。
我们在发射领域的领先地位、星链的经常性收⼊，以及  SI 基础设施业务的⼤幅扩张，共同驱动
了业务的显著运营杠杆效应。我们的资产负债表依然⾮常稳健，持续聚焦的资本配置使我们能够

保持加速创新的步伐，为三⼤业务板块的重⼤营收机遇提供有⼒⽀撑。
下⾯我将按业务板块逐⼀介绍财务情况。
太空业务营收 18 亿美元，环⽐增⻓ 84% ，同⽐增⻓ 6% ，得益于有利的客⼾结构。基于猎鹰和⻰
⻜船强劲的客⼾任务清单，我们有望实现全年稳健增⻓。
资本配置⽅⾯，我们持续投⼊星舰项⽬，加速猛禽发动机和⻜⾏器⽣产，推进  Gigabay 建设，
并在星际基地和卡纳维拉尔⻆同步推进多个发射台建设。此外，我们已确定美国航天发射能⼒下
⼀阶段发展的选址 —— 路易斯安那州。这些投资对于实现星舰所带来的成本与运⼒的跨越式提升
⾄关重要。
连接业务再度实现强劲营收增⻓和盈利表现，营收 52 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 69% ，环⽐增⻓ 22% 。
正如  Gwynne 所提，我们星链消费者订阅⽤⼾达到 1360 万，仅第三季度就新增 160 万，增⻓主
要来⾃成熟市场。本季度我们启动了  V5 套件的产能爬坡，有效缓解了库存紧张局⾯。我们在渠
道分发上进⾏了战略性管理，优先向零售渠道铺货以备战节假⽇旺季，预计这将推动第四季度激

活量提升。
星链消费者⽉均⽤⼾贡献收⼊（ ARPU ）环⽐增⻓ 5% ，达每⽉ 69 美元，受益于季度内有利的客
⼾结构。企业和政府收⼊同⽐增⻓ 91% 、环⽐增⻓ 18% ，主要得益于美国政府  Starshield 项⽬
的增⻓以及航空⾏业的持续强劲势头。航空业务依然是重要增⻓机遇，我们在全球多家⼤型航空
公司仍处于安装阶段早期，并已对主要合作伙伴 2027 年的加速激活路径有明确预判。
连接业务⽑利润同⽐翻倍，⽑利率达 55% ，其中包含约 3% 的⼀次性利好，主要源于本季度的关
税退税。剔除⼀次性因素后，调整后  EBITDA 利润率仍同⽐提升约 2% 。
展望未来，卫星路线图是连接板块产能和能⼒的核⼼驱动⼒。今年年底⾄ 2027 年全年，我们预
计每次星舰发射都将⼤批量部署  V3 宽带卫星，每次发射有效载荷所提供的下⾏容量将超过现有
猎鹰 9 号发射的 20 倍以上。与此同时，我们将于 2027 年起在星舰上启动星链移动  V2 卫星发射，
并利⽤我们新增的⽆线频谱资产，扩展直连终端服务，如  Gwynne 所⾔，成为美国主要移动运

营商。在星链宽带、星链移动和  Starshield 三⼤连接应⽤上，星舰的发射节奏预计将带来总容
量、覆盖范围和服务质量的跨越式提升。
超级智能（ SI ）再度成为本季度最突出的增⻓引擎。板块营收⼤幅提升⾄ 76 亿美元，环⽐增加
50 亿美元，同⽐增加 67 亿美元，主要由云服务协议的快速落地驱动。随着新增算⼒投⼊商业化
运营，我们⼤幅改善了板块的盈利结构，实现了强劲的运营杠杆效应。
截⾄第三季度末，我们的铭牌算⼒为 1.7 GW ， 10 ⽉初已有更多数据中⼼上线运营。

### Page 9

SI 解决⽅案与基础设施业务营收 70 亿美元，其中包含约于季度中期完成收购的  Cursor 贡献的
6.08 亿美元。即使剔除  Cursor ， SI 解决⽅案与基础设施营收仍较上季度环⽐增⻓ 192% ，主要
由新云服务协议激活驱动。⽬前有⼤量客⼾寻求算⼒，我们正积极扩充供给，以满⾜内外部需
求，并预计未来将持续拓展新客⼾和新合同。我们将持续评估算⼒资产在新⽼云服务协议及⾃有
SI 产品推理服务之间的最优⾼利润率⽤途。
当前市场的需求状况实属罕⻅ —— 以现⾏定价⽔平，新增算⼒的投资回收期不到⼀年。每⽡定价
⽬前略⾼于我们上季度讨论的 30 ⾄ 50 美元区间上限，进⼀步凸显了这些地⾯超级智能算⼒投资
的吸引⼒。我们将继续以需求为导向，在算⼒部署上保持审慎节奏，将半导体供应的资本规划与
近期客⼾需求紧密匹配。
我们当前的  SI 算⼒客⼾合同允许我们灵活管理算⼒资产，既可⽤于⾃有模型训练，也可在市场
供应持续收紧、价格有利的情况下，⽤于云服务协议创收。⼴告营收同⽐增⻓ 19% ，受益于我们

上季度（ Q2 ）推出的新⼴告技术平台所带来的增⻓势头。
SI 板块调整后  EBITDA 为 50 亿美元，环⽐增加 38 亿美元，同⽐增加 54 亿美元，主要由⾼利润率
云服务协议驱动。 SI 板块营业利润为 4.31 亿美元；但若剔除与  Cursor 收购相关的 12 亿美元股票
薪酬费⽤，该板块仅 Q3 就实现了 16 亿美元的营业利润。
就  Cursor 60 亿美元股权价值中基于股份的薪酬费⽤作进⼀步说明：收购时，其中 23 亿美元⽤于
留住核⼼员⼯及置换未⾏权股权奖励（包括关键管理⼈员的重新归属协议），这些股权奖励将在
约三年的归属期内确认为股票薪酬。
全公司第三季度资本⽀出为 196 亿美元，主要⽤于  SI 算⼒建设、星舰基础设施及卫星⽣产。
截⾄ 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇，我们的未完成订单总额达 630 亿美元，其中 175 亿美元确认为递延收⼊，约 420 亿
美元的未完成订单预计将在未来⼀年内确认。
展望未来，三⼤业务板块的需求均保持强劲，尤其是云服务业务 —— 我们有望如期实现此前宣布

的⽬标：以 12 ⽉份的⽉化收⼊为基础，今年年底年化收⼊达到 1000 亿美元。孟菲斯和南⿊⽂的
SI 基础设施持续上线，使我们对今年剩余时间内的加速建设路径有清晰预判。
因此，我们预计仅 10 ⽉份⼀个⽉，铭牌算⼒就有望接近 2.2 GW ；并正如  Elon 所强调的，到本
季度末，铭牌算⼒有望达到 2.5 ⾄ 3 GW 区间。由于本季度还将有更多算⼒上线，预计第四季度
资本⽀出将在 350 亿⾄ 400 亿美元区间。我们还预计， Q4 全公司⽑利率将与 Q2 总体相当，此前
Q3 的部分⼀次性利好及客⼾结构因素已作说明。
鉴于未来六个⽉算⼒合同管线的能⻅度提升，我们有条件就业务加速态势提供额外的前瞻性指
引。我们预计 2027 年第⼀季度全公司营收将在 330 亿⾄ 370 亿美元区间。
总结⽽⾔，公司财务⾯正处于深刻转型阶段。我们正在  SI 算⼒、星舰研发与⽣产，以及下⼀代
星链宽带和移动星座三⼤领域积极投⼊，这三个⽅向是我们认为⻓期回报最⾼的领域。这些都是
需求驱动型投资，所处市场在近期内具有清晰、可⻅的回报。

我们以严谨的态度构建未来基础设施，聚焦资本效率、强劲的投资资本回报，并在保持稳健投资
级资产负债表的同时，以⾜够的执⾏速度抓住当前机遇。多元化的盈利结构、极致的垂直整合能
⼒，以及市场对超级智能的强劲需求，共同⽀撑我们对未来发展路径充满信⼼。

其中⼀项我已公开提及的举措，是为燃⽓轮机铸造叶⽚和导向叶⽚。 SpaceX 已拥有极为先进的

⾯燃⽓轮机制造叶⽚，⽐起他们此前的挑战，可谓轻⽽易举。
我们从燃⽓轮机供应商了解到，叶⽚和导向叶⽚⽐其余部件的交付周期⻓ 12 ⾄ 18 个⽉，也就是
说，他们可以提前 12 ⾄ 18 个⽉交付除叶⽚之外的所有部件。铸造是⼀个真正的瓶颈，⽽且似乎
还在持续恶化。这意味着我们能够⽐任何⼈都提前 12 ⾄ 18 个⽉实现电⼒上线 —— 这种竞争优势
极难复制，除⾮有⼈另起炉灶建⼀家先进铸造⼚，否则⽆法在这⼀⽅⾯与  SpaceX 竞争。
我们也在探索天然⽓轮机之外的多种电⼒来源，包括太阳能、⻛能等所有可获取的能源。此外，
我们认为推理算⼒也有很⼤的机会 —— 如果在⼯⼚层⾯制造模块化推理计算单元，内置电⼒与冷
却系统，再将其分布式部署在全国乃⾄全球各地，效率将⼤幅提升。

升。
Bret Johnsen ：
Doug ，关于融资⽅⾯，我们的优势在于拥有投资级信⽤评级和稳健的资产负债表，⽬前正在与
多个债务资本来源进⾏接触。另⼀个重要的因素是投资回收期⾮常短，这意味着这⼀能⼒能够很
快实现⾃我造⾎。即便是本季度新增的算⼒，也将在明年上半年开始为融资提供⽀持。
因此，⾸先是经营性现⾦流，其次是投资级地位和强⼤资产负债表所带来的多元化债务市场融资
渠道，此外还有与供应商和客⼾在整个价值链上的合作机会。 SpaceX 做的事情从来都不轻松，
融资挑战也不例外。但我们⾮常有信⼼能够以审慎的⽅式稳步推进，并在整个过程中维持投资级
信⽤评级。
Elon Musk ：从我们在星舰及所有项⽬上的资本效率记录来看，你很难在全球找到另⼀家公司
